->Now, Shaunaka describes the timing for the Mula birth ritual:<-

> Now, I shall duly explain, for the benefit of those born under the Mula constellation,
> and for the sake of the wealth of the mother and father, and the peace of the lineage. ||3||
> On the twelfth day after birth, or on the birth-star day, or on another auspicious day,
> or in the eighth year, the wise and highly discerning person should perform the peace ritual. ||4||

Meaning—Following this, the sage Shaunaka says: for the benefit of a boy or girl born in the Mula <note>Mula is a lunar mansion or 'Nakshatra' often considered inauspicious if certain rituals are not performed</note> constellation, and for the peace of the mother, father, wealth, and lineage ||3|| the intelligent person should perform the peace ritual <note>Shanti</note> with great respect on the twelfth day from the child's birth, or on the return of the birth constellation, or on an auspicious day, or in the eighth year. ||4||

->Now, Vasistha describes the arrangement of the time and place for the duty:<-

> In a favorable time and a holy place, the wise one should have a pavilion constructed;
> it should be arranged in the Northeast, the East, or the Northern direction. ||5||
> The pavilion should be eight cubits <note>original: 'hastas', a measurement from the elbow to the fingertip</note> or four cubits on all sides,
> equipped with four doors and decorated with ceremonial arches and garlands. ||6||
> Outside of that, one should prepare the sacrificial fire-pit according to the path prescribed for planetary sacrifices.

Meaning—In a good season and at a holy site, learned men should build a pavilion <note>Mandapa</note>. This pavilion should be built in the Northeast corner of the house, or in the East, or in the North direction. ||5|| That pavilion should be a square of eight cubits or four cubits, featuring four doors and decorated with festive buntings <note>Vandanvar</note>. ||6|| Outside that pavilion, one should construct a fire-pit <note>Kunda</note> according to the sacrificial methods used for the planets.

->Now, the method of constructing the fire-pit:<-

> Outside the pavilion, one should have a square pit made;
> it should be two spans deep and have a rim of four finger-widths. ||7||

For Brahmins and Kshatriyas, it should be joined with three additional finger-widths.
For Vaishyas, an increase of two finger-widths; for Shudras, exactly one cubit. ||8||
The first <term>Mekhala (stepped tier)</term> there should be twelve finger-widths wide.
Its height should be four finger-widths all around. ||9||
Above that, there should be a <term>Vapra (raised edge or rampart)</term> four finger-widths high.
It should be properly extended by eight finger-widths all around. ||10||
Above that, a third Vapra should be made again.
It should be four finger-widths wide and of the same height. ||11||
The <term>Yoni (the lip or "womb" of the pit)</term> is in the western part, facing east and situated in the middle.
It is six finger-widths wide and twelve finger-widths long. ||12||
Raised at the back like an elephant, with a hole, and raised in the middle.
A fire pit endowed with such characteristics leads to the fulfillment of desired goals. ||13||
A fire pit where there is a deficiency or excess <note>meaning, incorrect measurements</note> grants many faults. ||14||

**Meaning—** Like the fire pit, one should make a square pit outside the pavilion. One should make such a pit that is two spans long, two spans wide, and four finger-widths deep. ||7|| However, for **Brahmins**, the pit should be made one cubit and four finger-widths long and wide. For **Kshatriyas**, it should be made one cubit and three finger-widths long and wide. For **Vaishyas**, a pit of one cubit and two finger-widths should be made. For **Shudras**, a pit only one cubit long and wide should be made. ||8|| Above that pit, first a <term>Mekhala (tier)</term> twelve finger-widths wide and four finger-widths high should be made. ||9|| Above that tier, a <term>Vapra (raised edge)</term> four finger-widths high and eight finger-widths wide should be made. ||10|| Above that, make a third Vapra again; a Vapra four finger-widths long and four finger-widths high should be made. ||11|| On the western side of the pit, facing the east...

The opening should be made such that a <term>Yoni</term><note>Literally "womb" or "source"; here it refers to a specific leaf-shaped or triangular protrusion at the edge of a ritual pit used for offerings.</note> is formed on the embankment of the <term>Kunda</term><note>A consecrated fire pit or altar used in Vedic rituals.</note>, measuring six fingers wide and twelve fingers long; that is, it should be shaped like a vulva. ||12|| That Yoni should be raised toward the back, with a hole in the center, and should possess a slight elevation in the middle. ### Now, the Five Nectars are described

> The Five Nectars, the Five Products of the Cow, and the five barks and sprouts.
> The bark and sprouts of the Udumbara, Vata, Ashvattha, and Plaksha trees. ||15||
> Yellow pigment, saffron, conch shell, elephant tusk, and bdellium resin.
> The roots of a hundred herbs, conch, the nine gems, and sacred earth. ||16||

<note>original: "पञ्चामृतं पंचगव्यं... नवरत्नानि मृत्तिका"</note>

Meaning—The <term>Panchamrita</term><note>The "Five Nectars" used in worship.</note>, the <term>Panchagavya</term><note>The "Five Products of the Cow" (milk, curd, ghee, urine, and dung) used for purification.</note>, the five barks, and the <term>Panchapallava</term><note>The "Five Leaves" from sacred trees used to decorate ritual pots.</note> should be gathered together. Cow's milk, clarified butter (ghee), yogurt, honey, and sugar—by mixing these items together...

...from which the <term>Panchamrita</term><note>The "Five Nectars" consisting of milk, yogurt, ghee, honey, and sugar.</note> is made.

Now, the **Five Products of the Cow** (<term>Panchagavya</term>) are described—The mixture of a cow's milk, clarified butter (ghee), curd, dung, and urine is called *Panchagavya*.

Now, the **Five Barks** (<term>Panchatvacha</term>) and **Five Leaves** (<term>Panchapallava</term>) are described—The bark of the Gular (Cluster Fig), Bargad (Banyan), Peepal (Sacred Fig), Pakad (White Fig), and Amra (Mango) trees is called **Panchatvak**. Collecting the leaves of these same five trees is called **Panchapallava**. ||15||

**Bezoar** (<term>Gorochana</term><note>A bright yellow pigment found in the gallbladders of cattle, used for tilaks and medicine.</note>), **Kumkum** (Roli), **Conch shell**, **Ivory**, **Guggul** (incense resin), the roots of a hundred medicinal herbs, the nine gems, and the **Eight Earths** (<term>Ashtamrittika</term>) should be collected. However, one should collect the Eight Earths while reciting the mantra "Syona Prithivi" <note>original: "स्योनापृथिवि"; a Vedic hymn to the Earth Goddess asking for a soft and thornless resting place.</note>. ||16||

### Now, the Eight Earths are described:

> From the stables of elephants and horses, from a chariot path, from an anthill, and from the confluence of rivers;
> From a lake, a cowshed, and from the gates of a King's palace and a city—these are the eight earths. ||17||

**Meaning**—The soil from an elephant stable, a horse stable, a public path, an anthill, the meeting point of rivers (or where two roads meet), a pond, a cowshed, the King's doorway, and the city gate—these are said to be the eight earths. ||17||

### Now, the Roots of the Hundred Herbs—according to Vashistha:

> The Bilva tree, Khadir, Vishnukranta, Punarnava;
> Devadaru, Jatamansi, Sahadevi, Mura, Shiva; ||18||
> Phalini, Bakula, Jati, Kala, and that known as Manjistha;
> Banyan, White Fig, Ashwanivara, Khadir, Mallika, and Arjuna; ||19||
> Madayanti, Mahajati, Neem, Ushira, and Turmeric;
> Sarpakshi, Tulsi, Raudra, Kuta, Pomegranate, and Champaka; ||20||
> Citron, Jaya, Rakta, Karnika, Einakanchana;
> Sevanti, Jackfruit, Grape, Vishwakshi, and White Mustard. ||21||

### On the Ten Herbs to be used in the absence of the One Hundred

> <note>original: "कुलमांसी हरिद्रे द्वे मुराशैलेयचन्दनम् । वचाचंपकहस्ताश्च सर्वौषध्यो दशैव हि ॥२९॥"</note>
> Spikenard, the two types of Turmeric, Mura, Stone-flower, and Sandalwood;
> Vacha, Champa, and Hasta—these ten are the "Universal Herbs." ||29||
> <note>original: "एषामभावे तु दश सर्वौषध्यो दशैव हि ॥३०॥"</note>
> In the absence of the former list, these ten alone serve as the Universal Herbs. ||30||

**Meaning—** Maulsari 1, Jatamansi <note>Spikenard</note> 2, Turmeric 3, Mango-Turmeric 4, Balchhad 5, Pashanbhed 6, Sandalwood 7, Vacha 8, Champa 9, Hasta 10. These ten herbs are called the "Universal Herbs" <term>Sarvaushadhi</term>. ||29|| If the one hundred herbs previously mentioned are not available, then these are designated as the Universal Herbs. ||30||

> Vishnukranta, Sahadevi, Tulsi, and Shatavari;
> One should take these roots, especially when others are unavailable. ||31||

**Meaning—** Vishnukranta, Sahadei, Tulsi, and Shatavari—of these four medicinal plants...

<header>With Hindi Commentary</header>

One should take the roots; if the ten medicinal herbs <note>original: "Dashoushadhi"</note> are unavailable, then these should be considered as the universal herbs. 31.

### Now, the Seven Seeds are described

Sesame, black gram, barley, paddy, wheat, and foxtail millet,
along with chickpeas; these are always the seven seeds. 32.

**Meaning—**1. Sesame, 2. Black gram <note>original: "Urad"</note>, 3. Barley, 4. Paddy <note>original: "Kukani"</note>, 5. Wheat, 6. Foxtail millet <note>original: "Priyangu"</note>, and 7. Chickpeas; these seven seeds are declared to be the best. 32.

### Now, the Nine Gems are described

Ruby, coral, pearl, cat's eye, and sapphire;
Diamond, emerald, topaz, and the one named zircon. 33.

**Meaning—**1. Ruby, 2. Coral, 3. Pearl, 4. Cat's eye <note>original: "Vaidurya-mani"</note>, 5. Sapphire, 6. Diamond, 7. Emerald <note>original: "Garutmat"</note>, 8. Topaz <note>original: "Pushparaj"</note>, and 9. Zircon <note>original: "Gomed" </note>; all these are called the gems. One should place these into the ritual water pot <note>term: "Kumbha" (a consecrated pitcher used in Vedic rituals)</note>. 33.

### Now, the Five Gems are described

Diamond, pearl, cat's eye, topaz, and sapphire;
These are declared the five gems, to be placed in the pots with mantras. 34.

**Meaning—**1. Diamond, 2. Pearl, 3. Cat's eye, 4. Topaz, 5. Sapphire; these are called the five gems. If the nine gems are not available, then one should certainly place these five gems into the ritual pot while reciting the sacred chants <note>term: "Mantras"</note>. 34.

### Now, the Measurement of the Idol

With a standard weight of gold, or half of that, or even half of that again;
One should fashion the image of Nirriti, avoiding any stinginess in wealth. 35.

**Meaning—**One should fashion the idol of the deity out of gold weighing one <term>Tola</term> <note>approximately 11.6 grams</note>, or six <term>Mashas</term> <note>half a Tola</note>, or three <term>Mashas</term>. However, one should make the idol according to one's financial capacity, without being miserly.

### Description of the Image according to other Texts—Shaunaka:

The wise person should have an image of the <term>Nakshatra Devata</term><note>The presiding deity of the lunar mansion or birth star</note> fashioned according to their own financial capacity, weighing either half a <term>Pala</term><note>A traditional unit of weight; half a Pala is approximately 16 Mashas or 24 grams</note> or a quarter of a Pala. ||36||

**Meaning—** One should have the form of the Star Deity made weighing sixteen <term>Mashas</term>, eight Mashas, four Mashas, or according to one's own means. ||36||

### Now, the value in the absence of a physical image:

If an image cannot be made, one should instead establish the value of the gold and perform the worship. Gold represents all the deities; Fire contains the essence of all gods. ||37||
The <term>Brahmin</term><note>Priest or learned scholar</note> embodies all the gods, and Lord <term>Hari</term><note>A name for Vishnu, the Preserver</note> is composed of all the divine beings.

**Meaning—** If one does not have the faith or means to create a golden image, then they should place the monetary value of that image in its stead and perform the worship. This is because all deities reside within gold, and Fire is also composed of all the gods. ||37|| Furthermore, know that the Brahmin is the embodiment of all gods, and Lord Vishnu is likewise the embodiment of all the divine.

### Now, the Procedure for Worship:

One should diligently offer sixteen or eight fine, white garments. ||38||
Afterward, one should invite the Brahmins after performing the <term>Swastivachana</term><note>A preliminary rite of chanting auspicious hymns</note>. Invite four, eight, twelve, or sixteen priests who are well-versed in the Vedas. ||39||
Among these, the Chief Priest is the most important for the worship of the image. In the four corners starting from the Northeast, place unblemished, red-colored water-pots filled with water. ||40||
These should be combined with the previously mentioned materials. One should worship the Brahmins individually with <term>Madhuparka</term><note>A ceremonial offering of honey, curd, and ghee</note> and other honors. ||41||

**Meaning—** Without laziness, one should give sixteen or eight fine white cloths. ||38|| Following this, one should formally invite the Brahmins along with the recitation of auspicious hymns for the sacrifice...

### Now, the Form of Mula is Described

The form of Mula should be represented as dark-complexioned, with a corpse as its vehicle,
holding a sword and a shield, fierce, with two faces and the countenance of a bull. (49)

one should also establish the planets and worship them with garments, scents, and other offerings. (50)

(49) With the sacrificial porridge, one should worship the deity of the Nakshatra to destroy sins, establish the positions of the planets, and perform worship with clothing, scents, and other materials. ## It was also stated by Shaunaka <note>Shaunaka is a celebrated ancient sage and Sanskrit grammarian to whom many ritual manuals are attributed.</note>

> Then, on the earth which has been sprinkled with water while reciting the *Punya* <note>original: "punyadi-mantra"; refers to auspicious or purifying hymns</note> and other mantras, one should place a water-jar that is smooth, red, and free from any flaws or cracks. ||51||
> One should fill it with pure water while reciting the *Akrishna* <note>original: "akrishnamula"; likely referring to the Rigvedic hymn beginning with 'A Krishnena'</note> hymn. The auspicious installation of the vessel should be performed with the mantra *A-kalasheshu* <note>original: "a-kalasheshu"; a Vedic verse used specifically for the ritual placement of jars</note>. ||52||

**Meaning—** Having looked at the water-jar, one should sprinkle it with water while reciting the *Punya* and other mantras. The jar and the water should be beautiful; it must not be broken or have any holes. ||51|| As stated by the *Akrishna* root-hymn, fill the jar with pure water and perform the auspicious installation of the vessel using the *A-kalasheshu* mantra. ||52||

> One should fill it with water from sacred pilgrimage sites <note>term: Tirtha-vari; water collected from holy rivers or springs</note> while reciting the mantra *Imam Me*. This should be done while including gold and auspicious sprouts. ||53||
> Having placed it atop a *Swastika* design, one should adorn it with the sprouts of "milk-trees" <note>term: Kshira-druma; trees that exude a milky sap, such as Banyan or Peepal, considered sacred</note>. Having cast down a *Drona* <note>note: a traditional measure of volume, roughly equivalent to 32 seers or 30kg</note> of grain, one should station the jar in the Northeast direction. ||54||
> Having cast five gems <note>term: Pancharatna; typically gold, diamond, sapphire, ruby, and pearl</note> into it along with all-healing herbs, and having honored it with scents, flowers, and other offerings, one should worship Lord Rudra therein. ||55||
> There, one should recite the *Pratiratha* hymn, the *Shatarudriya* chapter, the protection-mantras, the auspicious hymns, and the *Rakshoghna* <note>term: Rakshoghna; "Demon-slaying" mantras used for exorcism or protection</note> hymns while touching the vessel. ||56||

**Meaning—** One should fill the jar with water from sacred sites using the *Imam Me* mantra, including gold and five types of sprouts <note>term: Pancha-pallava; leaves from five specific sacred trees used in Hindu rituals</note>. ||53|| After performing the *Swastika* recitation, one should place the sprouts of milk-trees upon the jar. Having made a heap of thirty-seers of grain, one should install the jar upon it in the Northeast direction. ||54|| After placing the five gems and all the medicinal herbs into the jar, one should worship it with scents, flowers, unbroken rice, and other offerings, and then chant the *Shri Rudra* mantra. ||55|| Then, one should recite the Vedic hymns appropriate to it, the *Shatarudriya* text, the protection-mantras, the *Punyahavachana* <note>a rite for declaring the day auspicious</note>, and the *Rakshoghna* mantra...

In this same manner, one should perform the ritual of the water-pot <note>Ghat: a sacred vessel used in Vedic rituals to represent the deity</note> and the recitation of prayers <term>Japa: meditative repetition of a mantra</term> for the secondary presiding deity. The deity of the Purvashadha constellation is known as the secondary presiding deity (Pratyadhidevata). 62.

### Now, the Form of the Presiding Deity

> He of the massive body, the wielder of the thunderbolt, the lord of the planets, whose vehicle is an elephant;
> Having two arms, holding water and a lotus, and anointed with sandalwood paste. 63.

**Meaning**—Know the form of the secondary presiding deity to be thus: one whose body is large; who holds a thunderbolt; the king of the planets; whose mount is an elephant; who is like water; having two arms; holding a lotus in his hand; and whose body is smeared with sandalwood. 63.

### Now, the Method of Worship

> One should worship the primary and other deities with the mantras specified by their respective characteristics.
> Having bathed them with the five nectars, and having invoked them, one should then perform the worship. 64.
> With the sixteen types of offerings, or otherwise with the five types of offerings;
> With red sandalwood, scents, and flowers of black and white varieties. 65.
> With incense such as Meshshringa <note>literally "ram's horn," a specific aromatic resin</note> and likewise with clarified butter lamps;
> With offerings of wine, cakes, meat, and various foods. 66.

**Meaning**—One should worship the primary and secondary deities using the mantras specified for their signs. Bathe them with the five nectars <term>Panchamrita: a holy mixture of milk, curd, ghee, honey, and sugar</term>. After the invocation <term>Avahana: the ritual act of inviting the deity to reside in the image</term>, perform the worship. 64. Perform the worship using the sixteen-step ritual <term>Shodashopachara</term> or the five-step ritual <term>Panchopachara</term>, using red sandalwood, scents, and black and white flowers. 65. One should worship by offering incense like Meshshringa, lamps of clarified butter (ghee), and food offerings <term>Naivedya</term> including wine, cakes, meat, and other meals. 66.

### Now, the Prohibition of Fish and Meat for the Twice-Born

> Fish, meat, wine, and such things should be avoided for Brahmins.
> In place of wine, milk mixed with rock salt should be given. 67.
> In place of meat, one should provide rice-pudding mixed with salt.
> In the absence of the specified scents and materials, one should worship with whatever is available. 68.

<meta>This page continues the instructions for the sacrificial ritual (Homa), detailing the specific counts for offerings and the specific Vedic hymns to be recited.</meta>

<page-num>193</page-num>
<header>With Hindi Commentary</header>

One who has restrained their mind should perform the offering one thousand and eight times, or one hundred and eight times. This method of <term>Homa</term><note>A ritual where offerings are made into a consecrated fire.</note> is purified according to the different branches of the Vedas. ||74||
Using the mantra "Do not strike us, O Supreme" <note>original: "Moṣuṇaḥ parāpareti"</note> or "That which is of the Gods" <note>original: "yattedeve"</note>, one should offer milk-rice mixed with clarified butter one hundred and eight times. ||75||

**Meaning—** One should perform the sacrifice with fuel-sticks, sesame seeds, and sixty-day ripened rice while reciting the <term>Vyahriti</term><note>Sacred utterances: Bhur, Bhuvah, Svah.</note> mantras. One should chant the eight sentences beginning with "The root with progeny" <note>original: "mūlaṃ prajābhiḥ"</note> nine times. ||73|| One should perform the chant a fixed number of times, either one thousand and eight (1008) or one hundred and eight (108). This method of sacrifice is described; one should perform the chant according to the traditions of their own Vedic branch. ||74|| It should be performed with the mantra "Do not strike us, O Supreme" and the mantra "That which is of the Gods." ||75||

One should offer fuel-sticks, clarified butter, and sacrificial cooked rice <note>original: "charu"</note> according to one's own capacity and specific count. One should also perform offerings for the presiding and secondary deities using their respective mantras. ||76||
For the deities of the <term>Nakshatras</term><note>The lunar mansions or star constellations in Vedic astrology.</note>, one should offer milk-rice. Then, with the fifteen verses of the "Do thou act" <note>original: "kṛṇuṣva"</note> hymn, one should offer <term>Krusara</term><note>A dish made of rice and peas/lentils.</note>. ||77||

**Meaning—** One should sacrifice fuel-sticks, clarified butter, and other cooked offerings according to one's ability. The sacrifice should be performed with the mantras of the <term>Adhidevata</term> (presiding deity) and <term>Pratyadhidevata</term> (secondary deity). ||76|| For the sake of the Star Deities, one should offer rice pudding. With the "Do thou act" mantra, one should perform the <term>Chandi</term><note>A fierce form of the Goddess; here likely referring to a specific fire offering.</note> sacrifice fifteen times. ||77||

With the <term>Gayatri</term><note>The most sacred mantra of the Rigveda addressed to the Sun.</note> and the "Knower of all births" <note>original: "Jataveda"</note> mantras, twenty-eight times in sequence. Likewise for the Fire and the Lord of the Dwelling. ||78||
With the "Lord of the Field" <note>original: "Kshetrasya patina"</note> mantra and similarly the "Fire as Messenger" <note>original: "Agnidutam"</note> mantra. The wise one should offer fuel-sticks, clarified butter, and cooked rice in sequence with the <term>Sri Sukta</term><note>A hymn dedicated to Lakshmi, the goddess of prosperity.</note>. ||79||
The wise man should sacrifice either one hundred and eight times, or twenty-eight times in sequence, or even in groups of eight, according to his capacity. ||80||

# Female Horoscopy
->Page 194<-

**Meaning—** One should offer oblations into the fire continuously 28 times using the <term>Gayatri</term> <note>A highly revered Vedic chant for spiritual enlightenment</note> and <term>Jatavedas</term> <note>An epithet of Agni, the fire god, meaning "knower of all births"</note> mantras. Then, use the <term>Vastoshpati</term> <note>Mantra dedicated to the deity of the home/site</note> mantra. ||78|| Likewise, using the <term>Kshetrapata</term> <note>Deity of the field or land</note> and <term>Agnidutam</term> mantras, and the <term>Shri Sukta</term> <note>Hymn to the Goddess Lakshmi for prosperity</note>, the learned priest should offer <term>Samidha</term> <note>Sacrificial wood sticks</note> along with <term>Ghee</term> <note>Clarified butter</note> and <term>Charu</term> <note>A sacrificial preparation of boiled rice or grains</note> in the proper sequence. ||79|| The learned man should perform the <term>Havan</term> <note>Fire sacrifice</note> 108 times, or 28 times, or in groups of eight, according to his capability. ||80||

One should offer <term>Payasam</term> <note>Sweetened milk and rice pudding</note> thirteen times with the "Tvam Somena" mantra, and offer four portions of ghee with the "Yate Rudra" mantra. ||81||
One should offer ghee with the <term>Sruva</term> <note>A small wooden sacrificial ladle</note> using the <term>Mahavyahritis</term> <note>The sacred utterances: Bhur, Bhuvah, Svah</note> in sequence. After performing the <term>Svishtakrit</term> <note>An offering to Agni to correct any errors made during the ritual</note> sacrifice, one should then offer the <term>Prayashchitta</term> <note>Atonement or expiation</note> oblations. ||82||
The Preceptor (<term>Acharya</term>) or the Patron (<term>Yajamana</term>) should then offer the <term>Purnahuti</term> <note>The final "complete" offering signifying the end of the sacrifice</note> into the fire. After completing the remainder of the sacrifice, he should perform the <term>Vahni-Aropana</term> <note>The ritual concluding the fire ceremony</note>. ||83||

**Meaning—** Offer thirteen oblations with the "Tvam Somena" mantra and 4 oblations of ghee with the "Yate Rudra" mantra. ||81|| Perform the ghee sacrifice with the <term>Vyahritis</term> mentioned in the Vedas; after that, perform the <term>Svishtakrit</term> sacrifice and give the atonement oblations. ||82|| The Preceptor and the Patron should offer the final offering into the fire and perform the fire-concluding rites for the peace of the entire sacrifice. ||83||

One should perform the consecration of the water pot (<term>Kumbha</term>) and should consecrate it from the right side. To appease death, one should chant the <term>Tryambaka</term> <note>The "Three-Eyed One" or Mahamrityunjaya mantra for health and longevity</note> mantra. ||84||
Following the method prescribed for the <term>Rudra-Kumbha</term> <note>A ritual vessel dedicated to Lord Shiva</note>, one should touch the vessel while reciting the <term>Rudra</term> mantra. Incense, lamps, and food offerings (<term>Naivedya</term>) should be presented to the pots. ||85||
Then, one should reverently propitiate the Deity for the sake of the <term>Abhisheka</term> <note>Ritual bathing or anointing</note>. The ritual priests should then attend to the Patron, who is seated upon the <term>Bhadrasana</term> <note>A specific auspicious seating posture or ceremonial chair</note>. ||86||

# Female Horoscopy

May he pacify the defect arising from the *Moola* <note>The 19th lunar mansion, often considered unlucky for parents</note> constellation and the *Gandanta* <note>A spiritual or karmic "knot" occurring at the junction of fire and water signs</note> transition. ||92||

May the god who is the Lord of Treasures <note>Kubera</note>, who carries a sword and is carried by a man, remove the *Moola* defect from the mother, the father, and the child. ||95||

May the god who is the Lord of Animals <note>Shiva</note>, the wielder of the Pinaka bow who rides the bull, quickly remove the defects of *Ashlesha*, *Moola*, and *Gandanta*. ||96||

May the Lord of Obstacles <note>Ganesha</note>, the Protector of the Fields, the goddess Durga, the Guardians of the Directions, and the Nine Planets—all grantors of peace—pacify all defects. ||97||

Whatever beings exist in the three worlds, whether moving or stationary, along with Brahma, the Sun, and Vishnu—may they all remove the defect. ||98||

This ritual sprinkling <term>Abhisheka: a ceremonial pouring of water or consecrated liquids</term> for the two <note>likely the mother and child</note> is for the pacification of all defects; it fulfills all desires, is divine, and is the most auspicious of all auspicious things. ||99||

**Meaning:** One should perform the ritual sprinkling with these verses. ||89-99||

The wise practitioner should then perform the bath using two jars covered with cloth. Afterward, the subjects should wear white garments, white garlands, and be anointed with white sandalwood paste. ||100||

**Meaning:** The learned person should have them bathed using water jars covered by cloth; afterward, they should be made to wear white clothes and white garlands, and be anointed with excellent perfumes and pastes. ||100||

The patron of the sacrifice should satisfy the officiating priests and others with monetary gifts <term>Dakshina: a ritual donation or fee given to a priest</term>.

## Now, the Chapter on the Pacification of Ashlesha is Described

I shall now describe the pacification ritual for those born under the <term>Ashlesha<note>The ninth lunar mansion or Nakshatra, associated with serpents and often considered inauspicious if a child is born during certain parts of it.</note></term> star. One should perform the pacification <term>Homa<note>A consecrated fire ritual where offerings are made.</note></term> on the twelfth day after the child's birth. ||1||

If it is not possible on the twelfth day, one should perform the pacification on a favorable day when the birth star recurs. After bathing and applying fragrant oils, one should appoint the most excellent <term>Brahmins<note>Priests or scholars learned in Vedic rituals.</note></term> to conduct the ceremony. ||2||

**Meaning—** I am describing the pacification for a boy or girl born in the Ashlesha Nakshatra. On the twelfth day from the day the child is born, the pacification fire-offering (Homa) should be performed. ||1|| If it is not done on the twelfth day, then the pacification should be done on a favorable day when the birth star returns. One should bathe using medicinal pastes (ubtan) and then select the best Brahmins for the ritual. ||2||

If one has the wealth, five ritual water-pots (<term>Kumbhas</term>) should be established; if one lacks the means, then two. One pot is for the installation of the deity, and one is for the consecration of <term>Rudra<note>A fierce form of Lord Shiva, invoked here for protection and purification.</note></term>. ||3||

Following the procedure for <term>Moola-shanti<note>A similar pacification ritual for the Moola star; the text suggests using the same foundational steps here.</note></term>, one should place the pots and worship them. On a spot smeared with cow dung and beautified with heaps of grain, ||4||

one should create a lotus pattern there, measuring twenty-four finger-widths. The lotus should be made with rice of red, yellow, white, and black colors. ||5||

In the pericarp (center) of the lotus, one should place the syllables *Shrim* and *Hrim* <note>Sacred seed-syllables (Beeja Mantras) representing prosperity and the Great Goddess.</note> and establish the water-pots upon them. The auspicious installation of the jars should be done with the prayer beginning "Into the jars..." <note>original: "Aakalasheshu..." referring to a specific Vedic verse used for consecrating ritual vessels.</note> ||6||

**Meaning—** If one is wealthy, five pots should be established, and if one does not have the faith or means for five, then two pots should be established. One jar is for the installation of the Nakshatra deity, and one jar should be established for the chanting of the Rudra-deity. ||3|| Following the method of Moola-pacification, one should place medicinal herbs inside the pot and worship. Having smeared the ground with cow dung, the jar should be established upon a heap of grain. ||4|| There, one should make a lotus of grain measuring twenty-four finger-widths, or a lotus of rice...

One should establish five pots with all the necessary ritual materials. If one does not have the means for five, then two pots should be established. One should fashion an image of the lunar mansion <note>Nakshatra</note> in the shape of a serpent. Then, establish the image of Jupiter <note>Brihaspati</note> as the presiding deity <term>Adhidevata; the primary celestial power governing the rite</term> and the images of the Ancestors <note>Pitris</note> as the secondary deity <term>Pratyadhidevata; the supporting celestial power</term>. One should perform the worship using the mantra "Salutations to the Serpents" <note>original: "Namostu sarpebhyah"</note>.

### Now, the Meditation on the Ashlesha Mansion

The serpent has red eyes, two arms, and wears yellow garments. He holds a shield and a sword <note>interpreted from 'phalaka' and 'tikshna'</note>, and is adorned with divine ornaments. ||10||
**Meaning—** One should meditate upon the Ashlesha lunar mansion in this form. ||10||

### Now, the Procedure for the Ritual Acts

The practitioner or the presiding priest <term>Acharya</term> should perform the rite according to the path prescribed in the scriptures. Having completed the rites up to the Magha mansion <note>referring to the sequence of rituals</note> and taking the sacrificial offering <term>Havis</term> according to the scriptures: ||11||
One should offer the oblation into the fire with the mantra "This to the Serpents" <note>original: "Idam sarpebhyo"</note>, including the primary and secondary deities. This should be done either one hundred and eight (108) times or twenty-eight (28) times. ||12||
The remaining sacrificial acts should be completed in the same manner as the Mula mansion ritual. After finishing the fire sacrifice, one should perform the final "Full Offering" <term>Purnahuti; the concluding oblation of a sacrifice</term> and then the ritual of atonement <note>Sampatakam/Prayashchitta</note>. ||13||
**Meaning—** The priest or the patron <term>Yajamana</term> should perform the worship and fire sacrifice according to their specific Vedic branch. At the end of the sacrifice, after removing obstacles and bringing the sacrificial materials according to the scriptures: ||11||
Perform the fire sacrifice with the mantra "This to the Serpents." The count for the primary and secondary deities should be 108 or 28. ||12||
Complete all other remaining actions similar to the Mula mansion ritual. After finishing the fire sacrifice and the final offering, one must then perform the removal of sins/atonement. ||13||

### Now, the Consecration by Pouring Water

Pouring water from the ceremonial pot into the joined palms, one should perform the consecration <term>Abhisheka; a ritual bathing or sprinkling for purification</term>. This is done for the patron along with his wife and sons, as described previously. ||14||

## Now, the Consecration Mantra is stated:

**May the defects <note>Doshas: astrological flaws or spiritual impurities</note> affecting the mother, father, wealth, brothers, kinsmen, and the entire lineage of one born under the Ashlesha star be entirely removed.** ||15||

Meaning—May all the afflictions related to the mother, father, wealth, brothers, and relatives of a child born in the Ashlesha nakshatra <note>The 9th lunar mansion, associated with serpents</note> be destroyed. ||15||

## Now, the Protection Mantra:

**May the Ancestors <note>Pitaras: spirits of the departed ancestors</note> of all beings always protect the wealth and the kinsmen of those born under any star.** ||16||
**Salutations to you, O Lord of Serpents, the leader of the hosts of Nagas!** <note>original: "Sarpadhisha namastubhyam naganam cha ganadhipa"</note>
**Accept this offering of water given by me for the pacification of all misfortunes.** ||17||
**One should perform all rituals for Ashlesha in the same manner as for the Mula star, substituting the specific name of the defect.** ||18||

Meaning—Protection should be sought and an offering of water <term>Arghya: a ritual libation</term> should be given using this mantra. ||16||17|| All rituals should be performed for the Ashlesha nakshatra in the same way as for the Mula nakshatra, using its specific name. ||18||

## Now, Narada speaks on the defects of the Mula star:

**A girl born in Mula destroys her father-in-law; one born in Ashlesha <note>original: "Vyalaja"</note> destroys her offspring; one born in Jyeshtha <note>original: "Aindri"</note> destroys her husband's elder brother; and one born in Vishakha <note>original: "Dvidaivaja"</note> destroys her husband's younger brother.** ||19||
**However, if an extensive pacification ritual <term>Shanti: a ritual to avert evil</term> is performed, then no defect remains.**
**Thus, the pacification for a daughter born under the Serpent-star <note>Ashlesha</note> results in auspiciousness.** ||20||

Meaning—A girl born in the Mula nakshatra <note>The 19th lunar mansion</note> causes the destruction of her father-in-law, and...

--- Page 227 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of astrological afflictions caused by specific lunar mansions (Nakshatras) and begins the ritual instructions for neutralizing the "Gandanta" (junction point) dosha.</meta>

<page-num>203</page-num>
<header>With Hindi Commentary</header>

A girl born in the *Ashlesha* <note>The 9th lunar mansion, associated with serpents</note> nakshatra brings destruction to her husband's sister; one born in *Jyeshtha* <note>The 18th lunar mansion, associated with seniority</note> brings destruction to the elder brother; and one born in *Vishakha* <note>The 16th lunar mansion</note> brings destruction to the husband's younger brother. ||19|| By performing the complete peace-giving rituals (*Shanti*), all these defects are removed. The peace-giving rituals for a girl born in the nakshatras of *Mula*, *Jyeshtha*, *Ashlesha*, or *Vishakha* should be performed either at the time of birth or at the time of marriage. This is because a child born with the *Mula* defect brings affliction to their own family at the time of birth, and after marriage, they bring affliction to the father-in-law's family. The ritual for a girl born in the *Ashlesha* nakshatra is described as being comprehensive. ||20||

->### Now, the Ritual for the Three Types of Gandanta is Described<-

> I shall describe the *Gandanta* peace ritual, to be performed with devotion using the Moon's mantra. One should prepare a new bronze vessel weighing sixteen *palas*. ||21||
> Or it may be eight, four, or even two *palas* in weight, as is beautiful and fitting. In the center of it, place milk-rice (*payasam*) and a conch shell filled with fresh butter. ||22||
> Worship a silver image of the Moon with a thousand white flowers. The astrologer, dressed in white garments and adorned with white garlands, should perform the worship. ||23||

**Meaning**—I now describe the ritual for neutralizing the *Gandanta* defect—with devotion, using the Moon's mantra <note>original: "Imam Deva..."</note>, one should have a bronze vessel made weighing 53 *tolas* and 4 *mashas* <note>approximately 622 grams</note>, or a bronze vessel of 30 *tolas*. ||21|| Or a beautiful vessel of 26 *tolas* and 8 *mashas*, or 13 *tolas* and 4 *mashas*, or even 6 *tolas* and 8 *mashas* may be made. Fill the center of it with rice pudding (*kheer*), and inside the pudding, place a conch shell filled with butter. ||22|| Placing a silver Moon inside that, one should worship it with one thousand white flowers. The astrologer <term>Daivajña: one who knows the divine/fate</term> should be worshipped while wearing white clothes and a garland of white flowers. ||23||

<header>The Female Horoscope</header>

->Thinking "I am the Moon," one should perform the worship without laziness. One should chant the mantra one thousand times with faith and concentration. (24)<-
->With the mantra "May you grow full," one should perform the worship with a composed mind. To pacify the *Ganda* defect <note>Ganda-dosha: a spiritual blemish caused by being born during specific transitional periods between lunar mansions</note>, one should give the desired donation. (25)<-

**Meaning—**By using the mantra "I am the Moon" <note>original: "Somoham"</note>, one should cast aside laziness and perform worship. With faith, one should chant the mantra one thousand times. (24) By using the "May you grow full" <note>original: "Apyayasva"</note> mantra, one should certainly perform the worship and give a suitable donation to the priest for the pacification of the *Ganda* defect. (25)

->A white image of the Lord of Speech <note>Vagishvara, a form of Brahma or Brihaspati</note> along with a copper vessel should be given by a pure person to a scholar of the Vedas to pacify the *Ganda* defect. (26)<-
->For those born in defects other than the "un-eaten" *Mula* <note>Abhukta-mula: a particularly inauspicious first portion of the Mula star constellation</note>, the pacification ritual should be done at the end of the birth-impurity period <note>Sutaka: the period of ritual isolation following a birth</note> or on an auspicious day. Until the ritual is performed, one should not look upon the face of the daughter or son. (27)<-

**Meaning—**One should place a white-colored idol of the Lord of Speech in a copper vessel filled with clarified butter <note>Ghee</note> and give it to a Brahmin who knows the Vedas for the sake of pacifying the *Ganda* defect. (26) For children born in defects other than the "un-eaten" *Mula*, the pacification should be done at the end of the birth-impurity period or on an auspicious day. Until the peace-ritual is performed, the father should not look at the face of the child. (27)

### Now, the specific Ganda defects are described

->If born in the first quarter of Mula, Ashvini, or Magha, or in the fourth quarter of Revati, Jyeshtha, or Ashlesha, whether born at night, during the day, or at the two twilights, it surely brings destruction to the mother, the father, or to one's own body. (28)<-

**Meaning—**A child who is born in the first quarter <note>Charana: one of the four parts of a lunar mansion</note> of the constellations *Mula*, *Ashvini*, or *Magha*, or one who is born in the fourth quarter of the constellations *Revati*, *Jyeshtha*, or *Ashlesha*—
